5:T6bc,Sparsely attended which was ironic (or... you know) given the main thesis of the night was that there aren't enough people in the west who are knowledgeable on the current nuclear situation. 

The uncomfortable precise: the Cold War architecture that kept nuclear weapons in check (treaties, inspections) have expired. Now we are flying without any instruments. America's protective umbrella is rapidly decaying under Trump 2,0; and middle powers like the Netherlands which spent decades pushing for disarmament are edging back towards "actually, maybe we need nuclear protection". It was a noted, significant shift. 

The AI topic was where I started taking notes a bit faster. I was horrified at the bombing of an Iranian school -- and this was referenced in Shane Harris' talk earlier on AI in war. The argument wasn't that algorithms will _launch_ missiles -- that decision will (should) always remain with humans. What will happen (or is happening) is much more subtler: all the steps _leading up_ to that moment are increasingly handled by machines and algorithms.

If I've been worried about AI's impact on critical thinking and intellectual laziness, it's probably more apparent here.  What information exactly is the human with the big red button deciding with? You cannot, I reckon, outsource your thinking and then switch it back on for the HARDEST call imaginable. (NUC. LEAR. WEAP. ONS.)

The talk kept going back to 'ze Russians', as it were, and knowledge. Russia has a thriving community of nuclear strategists; the West has very, very, very few left. Someone said: go talk to the last generation who lived through deterrence as practitioners -- before they die. There was something so insane to hear that. 
6:T8f5,I loved Zabuzhko's energy and her give-absolutely-zero-fucks aura. She talked about how Ukrainians learned, over the generations, to make themselves invisible -- not to speak, not to show what you have, to survive by disappearing. Then she said, "Hey, the brutal irony is that when you go quiet, someone else fills the silence for you", and -- as we know -- Russia did exactly that, writing in Ukraine's story as a failed state for decades. Ukrainians only started pushing back in 2014 which, historically speaking is "five minutes ago".

There was also a Dutch-Ukrainian writer, Lisa Weeda, who spoke. Her angle was much more abrasive. She wasn't talking about Ukraine so much as talking about the West (or like, the Dutch, or "us" which was interesting because I am... not Dutch). Her thesis: the West has been handing Russia the microphone for so long that countries like Ukraine, Georgia, Moldova barely get a word in. Russia sees itself as an island with no outside word -- and yet we, the West, centre it in every conversation about the region. I think her speech was impassioned but a bit clumsy, but I liked her metaphor: the West has willingly been blindsided and other countries have lost oxygen. 

What was a decent thread throughout all the speeches and discussion was the idea that erasure starts with language. We redraw the Iron Curtain every time we lazily use "Eastern Europe".

One of the audience members asked: "How can you talk to Dutch people about Ukraine against the immense amount of Russian propaganda?" Zabuzhko said: forget out-arguing Russian propaganda -- you cannot outspend it. Go personal: a concert, a story, a book. She cited Notre Dame as an example -- it burns and the whole world mourns because it lives in people's personal heritage. The Sofia Cathedral in Kyiv is older and just as extraordinary but it doesn't live yet in the overall public consciousness. There is work to be done. 

There were some hopeful notes to this session. Dutch universities are now teaching Ukrainian, Slavic studies are widening beyond their Russia-fixation, and there are organisations embedding Ukrainian culture into Dutch life.
